const NOTES={y1w01:"---\nweek: 1\nstrand: number\ntitle: The Real Number System\nsubtitle: Natural numbers, whole numbers, integers, rational and irrational numbers \u2014 how to classify them, write them as decimals, order them and test closure.\noutcomes:\n  - Classify numbers as natural, whole, integer, rational or irrational, knowing that one number can belong to several sets.\n  - Use terminating and recurring decimals to tell rational numbers from irrational numbers.\n  - Convert a recurring decimal to a fraction in its lowest terms.\n  - Explain closure and test it with examples and counterexamples.\n  - Order real numbers and place them on a number line.\nkeywords: [natural number, whole number, integer, rational, irrational, real number, recurring decimal, closure, absolute value]\n---\n\n# Notes\n\n## What is a number system?\n\nA number can describe a quantity, a position, a measurement or a change. The number of learners in a class, a debt of GH\u20b540, half a litre of oil and the diagonal of a square tile all need numbers \u2014 but not the same **kind** of number. For example, 25 learners is a counting number, a debt of GH\u20b540 can be written as $-40$, half a litre is $\\frac{1}{2}$, and the exact diagonal of a square of side 1 is $\\sqrt{2}$.\n\n:::definition Sets and membership\nA **set** is a well-defined collection of objects, listed inside braces, for example $\\{1,2,3\\}$. The symbol $\\in$ means \u201cis a member of\u201d and $\\notin$ means \u201cis not a member of\u201d. So $3\\in\\{1,2,3\\}$ but $5\\notin\\{1,2,3\\}$. *Well-defined* means that anyone can decide, without opinion, whether an object belongs to the set.\n:::\n\nThe **real number system**, written $\\mathbb{R}$, contains every number that has a position on the number line: all the rational numbers and all the irrational numbers.\n\n## The number sets\n\n:::definition The five important sets\n- **Natural numbers** $\\mathbb{N}=\\{1,2,3,4,\\dots\\}$ \u2014 the counting numbers. In this course $0$ is **not** a natural number.\n- **Whole numbers** $W=\\{0,1,2,3,\\dots\\}$ \u2014 the natural numbers together with zero.\n- **Integers** $\\mathbb{Z}=\\{\\dots,-3,-2,-1,0,1,2,3,\\dots\\}$ \u2014 whole numbers and their negatives.\n- **Rational numbers** $\\mathbb{Q}$ \u2014 every number that can be written as $\\dfrac{a}{b}$, where $a$ and $b$ are integers and $b\\neq0$.\n- **Irrational numbers** \u2014 real numbers that **cannot** be written as $\\dfrac{a}{b}$; for example $\\sqrt{2}$, $\\sqrt{3}$ and $\\pi$.\n:::\n\nThe sets fit inside one another: every natural number is also a whole number, an integer, a rational number and a real number. We write\n\n$$\\mathbb{N}\\subset W\\subset\\mathbb{Z}\\subset\\mathbb{Q}\\subset\\mathbb{R}$$\n\nIrrational numbers are real, but they are **not** rational, so they sit outside $\\mathbb{Q}$.\n\n[[fig:w01-nested|Each set sits inside the next. The irrational numbers fill the part of $\\mathbb{R}$ outside $\\mathbb{Q}$.]]\n\nRational numbers include fractions such as $-\\dfrac{7}{4}$, integers such as $15$ (because $15=\\dfrac{15}{1}$), terminating decimals such as $0.6=\\dfrac{3}{5}$, percentages such as $25\\%=\\dfrac{1}{4}$, and recurring decimals such as $0.272727\\ldots$\n\n[[fig:w01-classify|To classify a number, first ask whether it can be written as a fraction of integers, then whether it is an integer, and then whether it is negative, zero or positive.]]\n\n:::warn A square root is not always irrational\n$\\sqrt{9}=3$ and $\\sqrt{49}=7$ are rational. The square root of a positive integer is irrational only when the integer is **not** a perfect square, for example $\\sqrt{7}$ or $\\sqrt{8}=2\\sqrt{2}$.\n:::\n\n## Rational and irrational decimals\n\nDividing the numerator of a fraction by its denominator gives its decimal form. The decimal of every rational number either **terminates** (stops) or **recurs** (a block of digits repeats for ever).\n\n- Terminating: $\\dfrac{3}{8}=0.375$\n- Recurring: $\\dfrac{1}{3}=0.333\\ldots=0.\\dot{3}$ and $\\dfrac{4}{11}=0.3636\\ldots=0.\\dot{3}\\dot{6}$\n\nA dot above a digit shows that it repeats; dots above the first and last digits of a block show that the whole block repeats.\n\nThe decimal of an irrational number **never ends and never repeats**: $\\pi=3.14159265\\ldots$ and $\\sqrt{2}=1.41421356\\ldots$ A calculator shows only an approximation.\n\n[[fig:w01-sqrt2|By Pythagoras\u2019 theorem the diagonal of a square of side 1 is $\\sqrt{1^2+1^2}=\\sqrt{2}$, an irrational length you can draw exactly.]]\n\n:::key Converting a recurring decimal to a fraction\nLet $x$ equal the decimal. Multiply by a power of 10 so that the repeating parts line up, then subtract to remove them. For $x=0.\\dot{3}$: $10x=3.333\\ldots$, so $10x-x=3$, giving $9x=3$ and $x=\\dfrac{1}{3}$.\n:::\n\n## Closure\n\n:::definition Closure\nA set $S$ is **closed** under an operation if, whenever $a$ and $b$ are in $S$, the result of the operation on $a$ and $b$ is also in $S$ \u2014 for **every** pair, not just some pairs.\n:::\n\nOne example that works does not prove closure; one **counterexample** is enough to disprove it. For example, the natural numbers are not closed under subtraction because $2-5=-3\\notin\\mathbb{N}$, and not closed under division because $1\\div2=\\dfrac{1}{2}\\notin\\mathbb{N}$.\n\n| Set | $+$ | $-$ | $\\times$ | $\\div$ (divisor $\\neq0$) |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| $\\mathbb{N}$ | Yes | No | Yes | No |\n| $W$ | Yes | No | Yes | No |\n| $\\mathbb{Z}$ | Yes | Yes | Yes | No |\n| $\\mathbb{Q}$ | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |\n| $\\mathbb{R}$ | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |\n\nThe irrational numbers are **not** closed under addition or multiplication: $\\sqrt{2}+(-\\sqrt{2})=0$ and $\\sqrt{2}\\times\\sqrt{2}=2$, and both answers are rational.\n\n:::warn Division by zero\nDivision by $0$ is undefined. Say \u201c$\\mathbb{Q}$ is closed under division by a **non-zero** rational number\u201d, never simply \u201cclosed under division\u201d.\n:::\n\n## Ordering numbers on the number line\n\nNumbers increase from left to right on a number line, so a number to the right is greater. Among negative numbers, the one nearer to zero is greater: $-2>-7$.\n\nThe **absolute value** $|x|$ is the distance of $x$ from $0$, so it is never negative: $|-6|=6$ and $|4|=4$.\n\nTo compare numbers, write them as decimals (or with a common denominator). Keep exact values such as $\\sqrt{2}$ until you need to compare, then use bounds: $1.4<\\sqrt{2}<1.5$ because $1.4^2=1.96<2<2.25=1.5^2$.\n\n[[fig:w01-numberline|The numbers of Worked Example 1.5 in order: $-2.3<-\\frac{7}{4}<0<1.3<\\sqrt{2}$.]]\n\n## Real numbers in daily life and a famous proof\n\nChoose the number set that suits the situation: counting chairs needs $\\mathbb{N}$ or $W$; profit and loss need $\\mathbb{Z}$; a measured length such as $2.75$ m is rational; the exact circumference $2\\pi r$ of a circle with rational radius $r\\neq0$ is irrational.\n\n[[fig:w01-thermometer|A thermometer uses negative and decimal values: $-5$ is an integer, while $18.5=\\frac{37}{2}$ is rational but not an integer.]]\n\n:::note Enrichment: why $\\sqrt{2}$ is irrational\nSuppose $\\sqrt{2}=\\dfrac{p}{q}$ in lowest terms, with integers $p$, $q$ and $q\\neq0$. Squaring gives $p^2=2q^2$, so $p^2$ is even, and therefore $p$ is even. Write $p=2k$. Then $4k^2=2q^2$, so $q^2=2k^2$ and $q$ is even too. But then $p$ and $q$ share the factor 2, which contradicts \u201clowest terms\u201d. So $\\sqrt{2}$ cannot be rational.\n:::\n\n# Worked examples\n\n:::example Classify and justify\nA data analyst records the numbers $-12$, $0$, $7$, $\\dfrac{5}{8}$, $\\sqrt{81}$, $\\sqrt{11}$, $0.\\dot{2}\\dot{7}$ and $\\pi$. Place each in the smallest set it belongs to: $\\mathbb{N}$, $W$, $\\mathbb{Z}$, $\\mathbb{Q}$ or irrational.\n---solution\n1. $-12$ is a negative integer, so its smallest set is $\\mathbb{Z}$.\n2. $0$ is whole but not natural, so its smallest set is $W$.\n3. $7$ is a counting number: $\\mathbb{N}$.\n4. $\\dfrac{5}{8}$ is a ratio of integers with denominator $8\\neq0$ and is not an integer: $\\mathbb{Q}$.\n5. $\\sqrt{81}=9$, a counting number: $\\mathbb{N}$.\n6. $\\sqrt{11}$ is irrational because $11$ is not a perfect square.\n7. $0.\\dot{2}\\dot{7}=0.2727\\ldots$ repeats the block $27$, so it is rational: $\\mathbb{Q}$.\n8. $\\pi$ is irrational: its decimal never ends and never repeats.\n---answer\n$\\mathbb{N}$: $7$, $\\sqrt{81}$. $W$: $0$. $\\mathbb{Z}$: $-12$. $\\mathbb{Q}$: $\\dfrac{5}{8}$, $0.\\dot{2}\\dot{7}$. Irrational: $\\sqrt{11}$, $\\pi$.\n:::\n\n:::example A recurring decimal as a fraction\nA bank calculation gives $0.363636\\ldots$ Express it as a fraction in its simplest form.\n---solution\n1. Let $x=0.363636\\ldots$ The repeating block, $36$, has two digits.\n2. Multiply by $100$: $100x=36.363636\\ldots$\n3. Subtract the first equation from the second; the repeating parts cancel:\n$$100x-x=36\\ \\Longrightarrow\\ 99x=36$$\n4. So $x=\\dfrac{36}{99}$. Divide numerator and denominator by $9$: $x=\\dfrac{4}{11}$.\n---answer\n$0.\\dot{3}\\dot{6}=\\dfrac{4}{11}$\n:::\n\n:::example A decimal that starts recurring later\nA measurement is recorded as $0.1\\dot{6}=0.16666\\ldots$ Find its exact value as a fraction.\n---solution\n1. Let $x=0.16666\\ldots$ The digit $1$ does not repeat; the digit $6$ does.\n2. Multiply by $10$ to move past the non-repeating digit: $10x=1.6666\\ldots$\n3. Multiply by $100$ to move one more place: $100x=16.6666\\ldots$\n4. Subtract:\n$$\\begin{aligned}100x&=16.666\\ldots\\\\10x&=\\phantom{1}1.666\\ldots\\\\100x-10x=90x&=15\\end{aligned}$$\n5. So $x=\\dfrac{15}{90}=\\dfrac{1}{6}$. Check: $1\\div6=0.1666\\ldots$ \u2713\n---answer\n$0.1\\dot{6}=\\dfrac{1}{6}$\n:::\n\n:::example Testing a closure claim\nA mathematics club says: \u201cThe whole numbers are closed under subtraction because $9-3=6$ is a whole number.\u201d Is the claim correct?\n---solution\n1. Closure needs the answer to stay in $W$ for **every** pair of whole numbers.\n2. One successful example does not prove this.\n3. Try the pair $3$ and $9$: $3-9=-6$, which is not a whole number.\n4. One counterexample is enough to disprove the claim.\n---answer\nThe claim is false: whole numbers are not closed under subtraction.\n:::\n\n:::example Ordering exact numbers\nA science class recorded the temperature changes $-2.3\\,^\\circ\\text{C}$, $-\\dfrac{7}{4}\\,^\\circ\\text{C}$, $0\\,^\\circ\\text{C}$, $\\sqrt{2}\\,^\\circ\\text{C}$ and $1.3\\,^\\circ\\text{C}$. Arrange them in ascending order.\n---solution\n1. Convert the fraction: $-\\dfrac{7}{4}=-1.75$.\n2. Estimate the surd: $1.4<\\sqrt{2}<1.5$, so $\\sqrt{2}\\approx1.414$.\n3. Negative numbers: $-2.3<-1.75$ because $-2.3$ is further to the left.\n4. Positive numbers: $1.3<\\sqrt{2}$ because $1.3^2=1.69<2$.\n---answer\n$-2.3<-\\dfrac{7}{4}<0<1.3<\\sqrt{2}$\n:::\n\n:::example Membership of several sets\nA laboratory records the temperatures $-5\\,^\\circ\\text{C}$, $0\\,^\\circ\\text{C}$ and $18.5\\,^\\circ\\text{C}$. List every set among $W$, $\\mathbb{Z}$, $\\mathbb{Q}$ and $\\mathbb{R}$ that each value belongs to.\n---solution\n1. Every value has a place on the number line, so all are real.\n2. $-5$ is an integer, so it is also rational $\\left(-5=\\dfrac{-5}{1}\\right)$ and real, but it is not whole.\n3. $0$ is whole, an integer, rational $\\left(0=\\dfrac{0}{1}\\right)$ and real.\n4. $18.5=\\dfrac{185}{10}=\\dfrac{37}{2}$ is rational and real, but not an integer.\n---answer\n$-5\\in\\mathbb{Z},\\mathbb{Q},\\mathbb{R}$; $\\ 0\\in W,\\mathbb{Z},\\mathbb{Q},\\mathbb{R}$; $\\ 18.5\\in\\mathbb{Q},\\mathbb{R}$ only.\n:::\n\n:::example Simplifying a surd to prove irrationality\nA learner says $\\sqrt{72}$ is rational because $72$ is an integer. Is this true?\n---solution\n1. Write $72$ as a perfect square times another integer: $72=36\\times2$.\n2. Use $\\sqrt{ab}=\\sqrt{a}\\,\\sqrt{b}$:\n$$\\sqrt{72}=\\sqrt{36}\\times\\sqrt{2}=6\\sqrt{2}$$\n3. If $6\\sqrt{2}$ were rational, dividing it by $6$ would make $\\sqrt{2}$ rational \u2014 but $\\sqrt{2}$ is irrational.\n---answer\n$\\sqrt{72}=6\\sqrt{2}$ is irrational, so the learner is wrong.\n:::\n\n# Summary\n\n:::formula Key facts for Week 1\n- $\\mathbb{N}\\subset W\\subset\\mathbb{Z}\\subset\\mathbb{Q}\\subset\\mathbb{R}$; irrational numbers are in $\\mathbb{R}$ but not in $\\mathbb{Q}$.\n- Rational: can be written $\\dfrac{a}{b}$ with integers $a$, $b$ and $b\\neq0$; its decimal terminates or recurs.\n- Irrational: its decimal never ends and never repeats, e.g. $\\sqrt{2}$, $\\pi$.\n- To change a recurring decimal to a fraction: multiply by $10$, $100,\\ldots$ and subtract.\n- A set is closed under an operation if the answer stays in the set for **every** pair.\n- $|x|$ is the distance from $0$, so $|x|\\ge0$.\n:::\n\n# Quiz\n\n:::quiz\n? The smallest of these sets that contains $-9$ is\n- whole numbers\n- natural numbers\n+ integers\n- irrational numbers\n= $-9$ is an integer but it is neither whole nor natural. Integers are also rational, but $\\mathbb{Z}$ is the smallest set listed.\n? Which number is irrational?\n- $\\sqrt{121}$\n- $0.125$\n+ $\\sqrt{15}$\n- $-\\dfrac{11}{7}$\n= $15$ is not a perfect square, so $\\sqrt{15}$ is irrational. $\\sqrt{121}=11$, $0.125=\\dfrac{1}{8}$ and $-\\dfrac{11}{7}$ are rational.\n? The fraction equal to $0.454545\\ldots$ in its lowest terms is\n- $\\dfrac{45}{10}$\n+ $\\dfrac{5}{11}$\n- $\\dfrac{9}{20}$\n- $\\dfrac{4}{11}$\n= Let $x=0.4545\\ldots$; then $100x-x=45$, so $99x=45$ and $x=\\dfrac{45}{99}=\\dfrac{5}{11}$.\n? Which statement about $\\mathbb{Z}$ is true?\n- It is closed under division by any non-zero integer.\n- It does not contain zero.\n- It contains all irrational numbers.\n+ It is closed under subtraction.\n= Subtracting one integer from another always gives an integer. Division need not: $5\\div2$ is not an integer.\n? Which list is in ascending order?\n- $-1.3,\\ -\\dfrac{3}{2},\\ 0,\\ \\sqrt{2}$\n+ $-\\dfrac{3}{2},\\ -1.3,\\ 0,\\ \\sqrt{2}$\n- $0,\\ -\\dfrac{3}{2},\\ -1.3,\\ \\sqrt{2}$\n- $-\\dfrac{3}{2},\\ 0,\\ -1.3,\\ \\sqrt{2}$\n= $-\\dfrac{3}{2}=-1.5$, and $-1.5<-1.3<0<\\sqrt{2}$.\n? If $x=0.0777\\ldots$, then $x$ as a fraction is\n+ $\\dfrac{7}{90}$\n- $\\dfrac{7}{99}$\n- $\\dfrac{7}{9}$\n- $\\dfrac{1}{7}$\n= $10x=0.777\\ldots$ and $100x=7.777\\ldots$; subtracting gives $90x=7$, so $x=\\dfrac{7}{90}$.\n? Which number is a whole number but not a natural number?\n- $-1$\n- $1$\n+ $0$\n- $\\dfrac{1}{2}$\n= $W$ includes $0$, but $\\mathbb{N}$ starts at $1$ in this course.\n? Which result shows that the irrational numbers are not closed under multiplication?\n- $\\sqrt{2}\\times\\sqrt{3}=\\sqrt{6}$\n- $\\sqrt{2}\\times1=\\sqrt{2}$\n- $\\sqrt{2}\\times2=2\\sqrt{2}$\n+ $\\sqrt{5}\\times\\sqrt{5}=5$\n= Both factors are irrational, but the product $5$ is rational.\n? A decimal that never ends and never repeats represents\n- an integer\n+ an irrational number\n- a natural number\n- a rational number\n= Irrational numbers are exactly the real numbers whose decimals neither terminate nor recur.\n? The value of $|-7|-|3-8|$ is\n- $-12$\n- $12$\n+ $2$\n- $-2$\n= $|-7|=7$ and $|3-8|=|-5|=5$, so the value is $7-5=2$.\n:::\n\n# Exercise\n\n:::exercise\n1. Explain why a terminating decimal can always be written as a fraction of integers. Use $0.0625$ as an example. || It has a finite number of decimal places, so it equals an integer over a power of 10: $0.0625=\\dfrac{625}{10000}=\\dfrac{1}{16}$.\n2. Place each number in its smallest set: $-\\sqrt{49}$, $\\sqrt{50}$, $0.\\dot{0}\\dot{9}$, $125\\%$, $-8$, $0$. || $-\\sqrt{49}=-7\\in\\mathbb{Z}$; $\\sqrt{50}=5\\sqrt{2}$ is irrational; $0.\\dot{0}\\dot{9}=\\dfrac{1}{11}\\in\\mathbb{Q}$; $125\\%=\\dfrac{5}{4}\\in\\mathbb{Q}$; $-8\\in\\mathbb{Z}$; $0\\in W$.\n3. Give two different counterexamples to show that the integers are not closed under division. || For example $1\\div2=\\dfrac{1}{2}$ and $7\\div3=\\dfrac{7}{3}$; neither answer is an integer.\n4. A diver starts at a depth of $-18$ m and rises $7.5$ m. Find the new position and classify it. || $-18+7.5=-10.5$ m, a rational number that is not an integer.\n5. Show that $\\sqrt{18}$ is irrational. || $\\sqrt{18}=\\sqrt{9\\times2}=3\\sqrt{2}$; if it were rational, $\\sqrt{2}=\\dfrac{\\sqrt{18}}{3}$ would be rational, which is false.\n6. Write $0.\\dot{1}\\dot{5}$ as a fraction in its lowest terms. || $99x=15$, so $x=\\dfrac{15}{99}=\\dfrac{5}{33}$.\n7. Arrange in ascending order: $-1.6$, $-\\sqrt{3}$, $1.7$, $\\sqrt{3}$, $\\dfrac{7}{4}$. || $-\\sqrt{3}<-1.6<1.7<\\sqrt{3}<\\dfrac{7}{4}$ (since $\\sqrt{3}\\approx1.732$ and $\\dfrac{7}{4}=1.75$).\n8. Evaluate $|-12|-|5-9|+|-3|$. || $12-4+3=11$\n:::\n\n# Theory questions\n\n:::theory A school laboratory records $-12$, $0$, $\\dfrac{7}{4}$, $\\sqrt{36}$ and $\\sqrt{10}$. Make a table showing the smallest set for each number, and give one real-life use of negative numbers.\n1. $-12$ is an integer (for example a loss, or a temperature below zero).\n2. $0$ is a whole number; it represents \u201cnone\u201d.\n3. $\\dfrac{7}{4}$ is rational but not an integer.\n4. $\\sqrt{36}=6$ is a natural number.\n5. $\\sqrt{10}$ is irrational because $10$ is not a perfect square.\n---answer\n$\\mathbb{Z}$: $-12$; $W$: $0$; $\\mathbb{Q}$: $\\dfrac{7}{4}$; $\\mathbb{N}$: $\\sqrt{36}$; irrational: $\\sqrt{10}$. Negative numbers record losses, debts and temperatures below zero.\n:::\n\n:::theory Express $0.\\dot{2}\\dot{7}$ and $0.58\\dot{3}$ as fractions in their simplest form, showing your working.\n1. Let $x=0.2727\\ldots$ Then $100x-x=27$, so $x=\\dfrac{27}{99}=\\dfrac{3}{11}$.\n2. Let $y=0.58333\\ldots$ Then $100y=58.333\\ldots$ and $1000y=583.333\\ldots$\n3. Subtract: $900y=525$, so $y=\\dfrac{525}{900}=\\dfrac{7}{12}$.\n---answer\n$0.\\dot{2}\\dot{7}=\\dfrac{3}{11}$ and $0.58\\dot{3}=\\dfrac{7}{12}$\n:::\n\n:::theory Explain the difference between checking a closure claim with one example and disproving it. Illustrate with the whole numbers under subtraction.\n1. Closure means that the result stays in the set for **all** pairs of elements.\n2. An example such as $8-3=5$ only shows one pair that works; it does not prove closure.\n3. The counterexample $3-8=-5\\notin W$ shows that $W$ is not closed under subtraction.\n---answer\nExamples cannot prove a statement about all pairs, but one counterexample disproves it; $W$ is not closed under subtraction.\n:::\n\n:::theory Explain why $\\sqrt{98}$ is irrational without using a calculator.\n1. $\\sqrt{98}=\\sqrt{49\\times2}=7\\sqrt{2}$.\n2. If $7\\sqrt{2}$ were rational, dividing by the non-zero rational number $7$ would make $\\sqrt{2}$ rational.\n3. But $\\sqrt{2}$ is irrational \u2014 a contradiction.\n---answer\n$\\sqrt{98}=7\\sqrt{2}$ is irrational.\n:::\n\n:::theory Arrange $-\\sqrt{4}$, $-1.75$, $0$, $\\dfrac{4}{3}$ and $\\sqrt{3}$ in ascending order, giving reasons.\n1. $-\\sqrt{4}=-2$.\n2. $\\dfrac{4}{3}=1.333\\ldots$, while $\\sqrt{3}$ lies between $1.7$ and $1.8$.\n3. Order the negatives, then zero, then the positives.\n---answer\n$-\\sqrt{4}<-1.75<0<\\dfrac{4}{3}<\\sqrt{3}$\n:::\n\n# Checklist\n\n:::checklist\n- I can name the five number sets and give examples of each.\n- I can explain why one number can belong to several sets.\n- I can decide whether a decimal is rational or irrational.\n- I can change a recurring decimal into a fraction.\n- I can test a closure claim and find a counterexample.\n- I can order real numbers and use absolute value.\n:::\n"};
